To achieve demographic parity, solve: \[ \Pr(\DecisionScore(\ModelInput) \geq \FirstGroupClassificationThreshold\mid \ProtectedGroupMembership = \FirstComparedGroupLabel) = \Pr(\DecisionScore(\ModelInput) \geq \SecondGroupClassificationThreshold\mid \ProtectedGroupMembership = \SecondComparedGroupLabel) \]

To achieve equal opportunity, solve: \[ \Pr(\DecisionScore(\ModelInput) \geq \FirstGroupClassificationThreshold\mid \ProtectedGroupMembership = \FirstComparedGroupLabel, \TrueOutcome= 1) = \Pr(\DecisionScore(\ModelInput) \geq \SecondGroupClassificationThreshold\mid \ProtectedGroupMembership = \SecondComparedGroupLabel, \TrueOutcome= 1) \]

For equalized odds, both true positive and false positive rate constraints must hold simultaneously. Varying a single deterministic threshold for a group moves its true positive rate and false positive rate together along one receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve, so when two groups have different ROC curves, no single cutoff per group may land on the same \((\text{FPR}, \text{TPR})\) pair. Postprocessing can instead randomize between two threshold rules within each group, which blends their error rates linearly and reaches interior \((\text{FPR}, \text{TPR})\) points where the two groups’ achievable regions overlap (Hardt et al. 2016). The resulting criterion constrains group-level probabilities, so validation must evaluate the randomized policy rather than only its component thresholds.

Hardt, Moritz, Eric Price, and Nathan Srebro. 2016. “Equality of Opportunity in Supervised Learning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 29: 3315–23.

Threshold adjustment still requires evaluating the other criteria. When base rates differ (that is, \(\Pr(\TrueOutcome=1 \mid \ProtectedGroupMembership=\FirstComparedGroupLabel) \neq \Pr(\TrueOutcome=1 \mid \ProtectedGroupMembership=\SecondComparedGroupLabel)\)), calibration and error-rate constraints can conflict under the conditions discussed earlier. The effect of a particular threshold policy must be measured on the joint score and label distribution; a base-rate difference alone does not determine its accuracy cost.

Lifting Group B’s approval rate to parity costs 4 points of accuracy.

The accuracy cost of a threshold policy becomes concrete on a separate credit model, distinct from the running loan example. Its score is a predicted default probability, so a lower score favors approval and each cutoff admits applicants below it. The scenario enforces demographic parity, the first threshold equation above, and prices the accuracy that enforcement costs.

Napkin Math 16.4: The fairness cost
Problem: Consider a credit model with 85 percent accuracy. Group A (majority) has a 20 percent default rate. Group B (minority) has a 40 percent default rate due to systemic factors. If demographic parity (equal approval rates) is enforced, what happens to accuracy?

Math:

This scenario holds Group A’s approval rate fixed and raises Group B’s rate to match it; demographic parity alone does not require that particular choice. The accuracy values are assumed held-out validation results, where the additional Group B approvals carry higher default risk.

  1. Unconstrained: Model approves everyone with predicted default prob < 30 percent.
    • Group A approval: 80 percent.
    • Group B approval: 60 percent.
    • Total Accuracy: 85 percent.
  2. Constrained (parity): Holding Group A’s rate fixed, approve Group B at 80 percent rate.
    • New threshold for Group B: Approve default prob < 50 percent.
    • The revised cutoff admits Group B applicants with higher predicted default risk than the original policy allowed.
    • New Total Accuracy: 81 percent.

Systems insight: This parity policy cost 4 percentage points of accuracy (about a 4.7 percent relative accuracy drop in this credit-scoring scenario). The drop is the fairness cost of this scenario, not a universal property of fairness constraints. Accuracy still measures prediction against the specified default labels; whether those labels capture the legitimate lending objective is a separate question. Engineers must report the predictive trade-off while making the target and policy assumptions explicit.

The 4 percentage points cost belongs to one parity policy on one prediction target. A different criterion moves the thresholds for a different reason, and the next scenario, which returns to the repayment-score convention, targets equal opportunity by comparing the score distributions among qualified applicants. Differential thresholds require access to sensitive attributes at inference time and raise concerns about explicit group-based treatment, which may itself be considered unfair or illegal in certain jurisdictions. The required thresholds depend on those distributions and the chosen criterion; a lower threshold for the minority group is not a universal fairness rule.

Napkin Math 16.5: Threshold for equal opportunity
Problem: Calculate group-specific thresholds required to equalize true positive rates (equal opportunity) across Group A and Group B in a credit scoring model.

Variables: Let the score \(\DecisionScore(\ModelInput)\) be an unbounded decision score, not a calibrated probability, and write Group A as \(\ProtectedGroupMembership=\FirstComparedGroupLabel\) and Group B as \(\ProtectedGroupMembership=\SecondComparedGroupLabel\). Assume \(\DecisionScore(\ModelInput)\mid(\TrueOutcome=1,\ProtectedGroupMembership=\FirstComparedGroupLabel)\sim\GaussianDistribution(0.72,0.15^2)\) and \(\DecisionScore(\ModelInput)\mid(\TrueOutcome=1,\ProtectedGroupMembership=\SecondComparedGroupLabel)\sim\GaussianDistribution(0.65,0.16^2)\). These are hypothetical positive-class distributions; no negative-class distribution or prevalence is specified.

Math:

  1. Single threshold: At \(\FirstGroupClassificationThreshold=\SecondGroupClassificationThreshold=0.60\), \(\text{TPR}_\FirstComparedGroupLabel=1-\StandardNormalCumulativeDistribution(-0.8)\approx0.788\) and \(\text{TPR}_\SecondComparedGroupLabel=1-\StandardNormalCumulativeDistribution(-0.3125)\approx0.623\), where \(\StandardNormalCumulativeDistribution\) is the standard normal cumulative distribution function.
  2. Equalizing thresholds: Preserve Group A’s threshold and match its standardized cutoff, \((\SecondGroupClassificationThreshold-0.65)/0.16=-0.8\). Thus \(\SecondGroupClassificationThreshold=0.522\) yields the same true positive rate.
  3. Unspecified costs: Lowering Group B’s threshold cannot decrease its false positive rate, but its magnitude requires \(\DecisionScore(\ModelInput)\mid(\TrueOutcome=0,\ProtectedGroupMembership=\SecondComparedGroupLabel)\). Precision also requires Group B’s prevalence. Neither quantity follows from the positive-class distributions alone.

Systems insight: Equal opportunity determines a threshold only relative to the stated score distributions and target rate. Before deployment, evaluate the resulting false positives, precision, and review workload on representative data. Threshold policies change decisions, not the underlying score calibration.

A separate illustrative loan scenario partitions applicants into Subgroup A (higher score distribution, blue band) and Subgroup B (lower score distribution, amber band), and the summary panels of figure 5 contrast two operating regimes. Applying a single global threshold (\(\ClassificationThreshold = 0.75\)) approves qualified applicants in Subgroup B at only 25 percent TPR compared to 75 percent for Subgroup A, a threefold opportunity disparity. Conversely, shifting to group-adapted thresholds (\(\FirstGroupClassificationThreshold= 0.75\) for Subgroup A and \(\SecondGroupClassificationThreshold= 0.45\) for Subgroup B) equalizes true positive rates at 75 percent. That policy requires the authorized decision component to access the relevant group attribute and log the governing threshold. Neither the attribute lookup nor the policy decision must run on the client, but the system must preserve evidence explaining why identical scores receive different decisions.

16.5.4 Fairness evidence across deployment tiers

Group-adapted thresholds and disaggregated audits assume that an authorized component can join predictions, demographic attributes, and outcomes, an assumption that breaks when execution leaves the data center. A federated keyboard model running across millions of phones, the regime of Archetype C (Federated MobileNet), never sends raw keystrokes or user demographic labels to the central server, so the fleet operator cannot run a central SQL query to check whether next-word accuracy drops on a protected subgroup. Fairness then becomes a deployment problem whose binding constraint is data visibility. A cloud platform with authorized access to demographic labels and outcomes can compute group-level metrics, run fairness-aware training, and audit post-hoc. A federated9 or on-device deployment instead needs an explicit evidence path, such as aggregate client statistics or a separately governed audit sample. Without sufficient evidence, the corresponding metric is unmeasurable; local execution alone does not make the audit impossible. The evidence available for fairness analysis narrows from cloud to TinyML (fairness row of table 4), and two consequences of that narrowing recur across deployments.

9 Federated learning and fairness: Google’s public Gboard trial illustrated training across devices without centralizing raw examples (Hard et al. 2018). Model updates alone do not necessarily reveal the demographic labels and outcomes needed for a fairness audit, so the communication cost, privacy protection, and statistical uncertainty of whatever evidence path replaces them must be evaluated for that design.

Hard, Andrew, Kanishka Rao, Rajiv Mathews, Swaroop Ramaswamy, Françoise Beaufays, Sean Augenstein, Hubert Eichner, Chloé Kiddon, and Daniel Ramage. 2018. “Federated Learning for Mobile Keyboard Prediction.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1811.03604.
Dwork, Cynthia, Moritz Hardt, Toniann Pitassi, Omer Reingold, and Richard Zemel. 2012. “Fairness Through Awareness.” Proceedings of the 3rd Innovations in Theoretical Computer Science Conference, 214–26. https://doi.org/10.1145/2090236.2090255.

The first consequence concerns decision policy. Equal aggregate accuracy across groups does not establish equal opportunity under a uniform threshold, as the illustrative gap in figure 5 demonstrates. A deployment must select and evaluate a policy against its approved criterion; group-specific thresholds are one possible intervention, not a requirement imposed by edge execution. The second consequence sharpens the personalization conflict raised in section 16.4.1. Local adaptation aims to improve a user’s utility, whereas individual fairness requires similar individuals to receive similar decisions under a task-relevant similarity metric (Dwork et al. 2012). Personalization does not establish that requirement, and adaptation on sparse or unrepresentative observations can reinforce existing gaps. When the relevant evidence stays local, fleet operators need an authorized way to assess the resulting disparities. Section 16.9.4.1 analyzes the feedback mechanism and its intervention points; the deployment consequence is that a fleet which cannot centralize outcomes must budget an explicit evidence path for detecting it.

That evidence path creates a further responsible AI tension. Keeping sensitive data local reduces centralized exposure, while fleet-level audits still need adequate group statistics. Privacy architecture (section 16.6.2) determines which records or aggregates may cross the boundary and under what protections; once an authorized evidence stream reaches the operator, the next engineering problem is turning those records into continuous bias detection and production monitoring.

16.5.5 Bias detection and monitoring

Periodic pre-release audits cannot catch disparities that emerge between reviews when traffic or applicant populations shift. Suppose a nationally deployed credit model begins rejecting applicants from a specific ZIP code at twice its previous rate. Monitoring can expose that change before a scheduled audit, but the rate alone does not establish its cause. An investigator must check population changes, data quality, and decision policy against the approved fairness criteria. Bias detection turns formal definitions into operational telemetry, using slice-based analysis to identify subpopulations whose outcomes warrant examination.

Measurement costs and intervention points

The criteria of section 16.5.1 define what a disparity is and how to test one. Computing them continuously on a production system that serves thousands of predictions per second is where the engineering cost appears. That cost has computational, data, and organizational parts, and together they decide which metrics a system can afford to monitor.

Measurement overhead arises because computing group-specific metrics requires maintaining separate statistics for each protected group: for \(\MonitoredGroupCount\) groups and \(\MonitoredFairnessMetricCount\) metrics, this adds \(\mathcal{O}(\MonitoredGroupCount \MonitoredFairnessMetricCount)\) counters and \(\mathcal{O}(\MonitoredGroupCount \MonitoredFairnessMetricCount)\) statistical tests per evaluation cycle. Streaming counters can update cheaply per prediction while statistical tests run at a separate evaluation interval, so profiling the number of groups, windows, and tests, rather than request rate alone, determines whether sampling or asynchronous aggregation is needed. Those counters also depend on which fields the metric consumes, because error-rate criteria need outcome labels (\(\TrueOutcome\)) alongside sensitive attributes \((\ProtectedGroupMembership)\) across a sample that supports the intended population claim, whereas demographic parity needs group membership and decisions without outcomes. Availability of those sensitive attributes requires an explicit governance and engineering choice among authorized direct collection, restricted audit records, encrypted or differentially private group statistics, or an offline sample. When inferred attributes are used instead, the audit must evaluate inference errors rather than treat the inferred groups as known. When the evidence cannot support a metric, the system must report that limitation rather than interpret missing measurements as fair behavior.

Selecting acceptable disparity thresholds then demands domain expertise and stakeholder input, since the four-fifths rule is a screening convention from U.S. employment selection rather than a universal legal test for lending or fraud detection. Thresholds must reflect context-specific harm assessments, statistical uncertainty, and documented legal rationale. Because distribution shift, feedback loops, or model updates can erode fairness over time, temporal stability further requires monitoring those thresholds across evaluation windows. Automated alerting (for example, “alert if \(|\text{SPD}| > 0.15\) for 7 consecutive days”) defines when an owner receives a signal. The persistence window reduces sensitivity to brief fluctuations at the cost of response delay, so its acceptability depends on the harm and exposure during that interval, as section 16.9.3 develops.

Offline and CI-stage audits are the first place these metrics run, and open-source toolkits such as Fairlearn package the per-group bookkeeping (Bird et al. 2020), as in the audit of listing 1. Applied to the running loan example, that audit’s per-group table would report approval rates of 0.70 for Group A and 0.40 for Group B, the disparate impact ratio of 0.57 that falls below the 0.8 screening threshold in table 6. That comparison is an audit signal, not a lending-law verdict. The architecture must supply aligned decisions and group membership through an authorized audit path. Metrics that require outcome labels must use the relevant observed outcomes rather than substitute past decisions; an offline audit does not require exposing group attributes throughout runtime serving.

Bird, Sarah, Miro Dudı́k, Richard Edgar, Brandon Horn, Roman Lutz, Vanessa Milan, Mehrnoosh Sameki, Hanna Wallach, and Kathleen Walker. 2020. “Fairlearn: A Toolkit for Assessing and Improving Fairness in AI.” Microsoft Technical Report MSR-TR-2020-32.

Where sensitive attributes are unavailable at inference time, as is common in mobile, federated, and embedded systems, group-specific thresholds may be infeasible. Restricted offline audits can still guide data changes, retraining, or a revised policy that does not require an attribute lookup on each request.

Fairness interventions may be applied at different points in the pipeline, but each comes with system-level implications. Preprocessing methods change the training distribution through sampling, reweighting, or augmentation. Their data requirements depend on the method; group-targeted reweighting needs group information, while feature augmentation also needs access to the affected examples. Versioning and lineage must preserve which transformation produced the training data. In-processing approaches instead embed fairness constraints directly into optimization. They require support for the chosen loss or constrained solver and may add training work or validation requirements.

↩︎ Feature versioning and the lineage records that trace each training input are established in Feature versioning and lineage.

At the serving layer, postprocessing methods apply an approved decision rule to model outputs. Group-specific thresholds require the component choosing the threshold to obtain authorized group information; other policy rules may use different inputs. The dual-threshold policy in figure 5 (\(\FirstGroupClassificationThreshold= 0.75\), \(\SecondGroupClassificationThreshold= 0.45\)) therefore needs a controlled attribute lookup and a record of the applied policy, but not propagation of the attribute through every routing and inference component. Access controls should limit that exposure. Validation must evaluate the resulting decisions, operational stability, and applicable restrictions on attribute use.

Multicalibration (section 16.5.2.3) can run in backend training infrastructure even when the resulting model serves on a constrained endpoint, but a training-time result does not establish calibration after deployment conditions change. If telemetry cannot support renewed subgroup audits, the operator must record that evidence limitation and restrict the approved operating envelope accordingly.

Mitigation also decays after launch. A fairness-aware model may degrade if retraining pipelines omit fairness checks, if logging cannot track subgroup outcomes, or if user feedback introduces biases absent from the training distribution, so retraining protocols need subgroup-labeled validation data under data-governance review. Deciding which disparity to remediate, and at what cost to other objectives, remains a stakeholder decision (section 16.9.4.3). The architecture’s job is to make each disparity visible, in time, to the owner who must make that decision.

Real-time fairness monitoring architecture

Delivering that visibility without stalling live inference requires decoupling the serving path from the governance path (figure 6). A synchronous request path enforces access control, data minimization, and low-latency inference, while an asynchronous governance tier ingests bounded execution evidence and delayed labels to drive fairness auditing, post-hoc explanations, and owner-governed release gates.

The request path applies access controls and data minimization before inference and records only the evidence needed for the approved monitoring policy. Group-level fairness analysis and expensive explanations can run asynchronously; synchronous checks must fit the serving service-level objective (SLO). Delayed labels enter the analysis path when outcomes become available, and a named owner approves model or policy changes.

Real-time fairness monitoring updates prediction-only metrics such as demographic parity as decisions arrive, maintaining rolling statistics across protected groups. Label-dependent metrics such as equalized odds and equality of opportunity update when delayed outcomes or audited labels arrive. Groups whose labeled windows are too small must remain visible as having insufficient evidence, with a defined collection or escalation policy. The system flags supported disparities that exceed configured thresholds and separately reports gaps in coverage. Storage and compute requirements depend on the number of groups, decision segments, and retention windows.

The explanation engine generates evidence for the audience and decision under review. Its budget depends on the method and model; exact model-specific attribution may be feasible where exhaustive subset evaluation is not. Approximation, caching, selective generation, and asynchronous execution offer different cost and delivery-time trade-offs. The monitoring part of this architecture collects predictions and computes rolling group metrics with threshold alerts (listing 2). The listing omits delayed outcome ingestion, the full equalized-odds calculation, and explanation generation, so it is a partial implementation rather than a complete governance service.

This simplified pattern separates metric availability from metric values, because an unavailable comparison is not a zero disparity. A production monitor must also enforce required group coverage, sample-size policies, and freshness before interpreting a result. The overhead ranges summarized in table 5 explain why fairness monitoring, privacy transformations, and explanation generation need explicit capacity budgets. Request metadata collection may be synchronous, while metric aggregation, delayed-label analysis, and explanation generation can run asynchronously under their own freshness requirements. These overheads must be balanced against reliability and compliance requirements when designing production systems.

At a 5 percent false-alarm rate, 150 metrics raise about 7.5 spurious alerts per interval.

At the scale of a global production fleet, fairness monitoring becomes a substantial data engineering challenge. This sizing scenario assumes roughly 864 million inferences per day at 10,000 queries/s across 50 demographic subgroups, with 150 metrics (false positive rate, true positive rate, and calibration error for each of the 50 groups). A 1 percent sampling rate yields 8.64 million monitoring events daily. At an assumed 200 bytes per record, those events require approximately 1.7 GB per day before additional audit records or storage overhead. Actual record contents and retention follow the approved measurement and privacy requirements; outcome-dependent metrics also require labels that may arrive later. The monitoring infrastructure itself must therefore be reliable, secure, and cost-effective. With 150 active metrics, an illustrative per-metric false-alarm probability of 5 percent would yield roughly 7.5 false alerts per evaluation interval if every tested null hypothesis is true. This expected count follows by summing false-alarm probabilities and does not require independence. Production thresholds must instead reflect subgroup denominators, delayed labels, repeated testing, confidence intervals, minimum detectable effects, and correction for multiple comparisons. A threshold crossing opens an investigation; it does not by itself prove discrimination or justify automatic retraining.

↩︎ False alarms, the spurious threshold crossings that accumulate as a fleet multiplies monitored metrics, are established in The alert fatigue problem.

In C³ terms, this monitoring load is communication and coordination added to the fleet. Every sampled record must travel from a serving replica to the aggregation tier, and every alert must reach an owner who can act before the harm it signals compounds. The deployment tier sets how long that path is. A cloud service aggregates telemetry continuously, subject to storage and privacy budgets. An intermittently connected client keeps bounded local records until it next synchronizes, and a safety-critical edge system needs local checks that can trigger fallback within the decision deadline because the fleet cannot respond in time. A disconnected TinyML endpoint may deliver its evidence only at a servicing visit, and correcting its model may require a firmware update or physical replacement. Those differences change the evidence delay and remediation path, not the requirement to name an owner for each monitored obligation.

Checkpoint 16.1: Putting bias detection into production

The bias-detection and monitoring sections turned static fairness definitions into live telemetry: slice-based detection with Fairlearn, CI-stage audits, production monitoring of demographic parity and equalized odds, the evidence paths that federated and on-device deployments require, and the distinction between observing a disparity and remediating it.

Detecting the disparity

From signal to action

Self-Check: Question

  1. A loan approval model evaluates two demographic groups (Group A and Group B). Group A has a baseline approval rate of \(70\%\) (\(\text{PPV} = 57.1\%\)), while Group B has a baseline approval rate of \(40\%\) (\(\text{PPV} = 75.0\%\)). If the engineering team lowers the decision threshold for Group B to enforce demographic parity (\(70\%\) approval for both groups), what is the immediate system-level consequence?

    1. Group B’s positive predictive value increases while overall model accuracy remains completely unchanged
    2. Lowering the threshold admits additional applicants with lower predicted repayment scores; the effects on approval precision and overall accuracy must be evaluated rather than inferred from approval rates alone.
    3. The model automatically satisfies equalized odds without any change to false positive rates
    4. The underlying true default base rates of both populations become mathematically equalized

  2. For risk scores, the chapter’s score-balance impossibility result combines calibration within each group with equal mean scores across groups among positive outcomes and, separately, among negative outcomes. Which cases escape the stated incompatibility?

    1. Whenever the training dataset contains more than \(10^6\) balanced records across all demographic slices
    2. Whenever the model is trained with \(L_2\) regularization and post-hoc isotonic calibration
    3. Whenever demographic parity is explicitly enforced via multi-task adversarial training
    4. Only in degenerate or trivial cases, such as perfect prediction (zero error) or identical base rates across groups

  3. Explain how single-axis fairness audits can enable ‘fairness gerrymandering’ and describe the evaluation strategy required to detect it.

  4. Which statement correctly differentiates Preprocessing, In-processing, and Postprocessing fairness interventions with respect to ML system infrastructure requirements?

    1. Preprocessing modifies serving thresholds at inference, In-processing requires anonymizing logs, and Postprocessing alters training labels in feature stores
    2. All three methods require identical runtime compute overhead on the live model serving path
    3. Preprocessing is only applicable to TinyML devices, while In-processing and Postprocessing can only run in cloud clusters
    4. Preprocessing changes training data and needs lineage; in-processing changes training optimization; group-specific threshold postprocessing requires authorized group information at the component applying the decision rule.

  5. A streaming loan-approval service records authorized group attributes and decisions immediately, but repayment outcomes arrive months later. Why can demographic-parity statistics update earlier than equalized-odds statistics?

    1. Demographic Parity depends only on model predictions and input demographics available at inference time, whereas Equalized Odds requires true outcome labels that arrive with substantial real-world latency
    2. Demographic Parity requires calculating matrix inverses that execute faster on GPU tensor cores
    3. Equalized Odds is a convex optimization problem that cannot be evaluated on streaming CPU worker nodes
    4. Demographic Parity violates privacy regulations if processed asynchronously in background databases

  6. A production ML fleet serving 864 million daily inferences tracks 150 distinct subgroup fairness metrics. With an unadjusted per-metric false alarm rate of \(5\%\), the system experiences roughly 7.5 false alerts per evaluation interval. Explain the operational consequence of this false alarm volume and describe how the monitoring architecture should mitigate it.

16.6 Privacy and Data Governance

Every fairness audit and monitor consumes demographic evidence, and that evidence is itself sensitive data. The threat models and privacy mechanisms of Security & Privacy provide the technical controls; governance defines the evidence, ownership, and lifecycle interfaces that production systems require when inheriting them. A deletion workflow must identify which records and derived artifacts fall within an applicable obligation, such as an erasure requirement under the GDPR (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2016). Removing a database row may leave replicas, backups, or trained models to address. Where removal of training influence is required, lineage, retraining, validation, deployment, and audit become part of the same workflow.

Governing these risks coordinates two classes of control that are easy to conflate. Privacy mechanisms bound specified information exposures, while machine unlearning (section 16.6.4) targets the influence a record retains in trained weights under a stated removal criterion. A privacy guarantee does not by itself remove a record from the training history, so the assurance case must distinguish these obligations and supply evidence for each. Robustness and generative-system controls receive the same evidence-and-ownership treatment in section 16.8.

16.6.1 Core data governance principles

That coordination starts at data ingestion, where a compliance rule demanding data minimization can collide directly with a fairness audit that needs a protected demographic field. Whereas technical privacy mechanisms constrain exposure and leakage, data governance determines whether collecting the field is justified, who may access it, and how long the audit may retain it. This decision connects privacy to fairness (section 16.5.1) and to transparency (section 16.7.1). Group-based monitoring needs evidence about group membership, but the audit design determines whether operators receive individual fields, restricted audit records, or aggregate statistics. Explainability methods can also reveal information about training examples, while detailed disclosure can conflict with individual privacy. Responsible AI systems must justify the evidence collected and limit its exposure while retaining enough information to evaluate the chosen requirements.

↩︎ Federated learning, encrypted computation, and synthetic data, each bounding who can observe raw records, are established in Privacy-preserving data techniques.

Carlini, N., F. Tramer, E. Wallace, M. Jagielski, A. Herbert-Voss, K. Lee, A. Roberts, et al. 2021. “Extracting Training Data from Large Language Models.” 30th USENIX Security Symposium (USENIX Security 21), 2633–50.

Because training and personalization rely on personal data, privacy is a design constraint across the whole system lifecycle, and its central difficulty is the tension between data utility and individual protection. Rich, high-resolution datasets can enhance model accuracy and adaptability but also heighten the risk of exposing sensitive information, particularly when datasets are aggregated or linked with external sources. For example, large language models trained on broad text corpora have been shown to memorize specific strings that can later be retrieved through model queries or adversarial prompting (Carlini et al. 2021).

↩︎ The GPT-2 extraction attack, which recovered verbatim training records through ordinary generated outputs, is established in GPT-2 data extraction case study.

Privacy risks also depend on how records are linked over time and across sources. A wearable device’s heart-rate or location record can already be sensitive; combining physiological and behavioral signals can reveal additional details about a user’s health, routines, or relationships. Limited visibility or control over processing, retention, and transmission makes it harder for users to understand or contest these uses.

Addressing these challenges requires understanding privacy as a system principle that entails robust data governance. This includes defining what data is collected, under what conditions, and with what degree of consent and transparency. Foundational data engineering practices, including data validation, schema management, versioning, and lineage tracking, provide the technical infrastructure for implementing these governance requirements. Responsible governance requires attention to labeling practices, access controls, logging infrastructure, and compliance with jurisdictional requirements. These mechanisms serve to constrain how data flows through a system and to document accountability for its use.

Weak data governance can obscure both quality defects and privacy exposures, but they require different evidence. In clinical applications, missing values or demographic skew can compromise predictive performance; neither alone establishes a privacy violation. Privacy assessment must also examine identifiability, access, and disclosure. Documentation should make these distinct risks traceable to the data practices that create them.

Privacy is not solely the concern of isolated algorithms or data processors; like fairness, it must be addressed as a structural property of the system. Decisions about consent collection, data retention, model design, and auditability all contribute to the privacy posture of a machine learning pipeline. This requires anticipating risks during inference and continuous operation as well as during training. Threats such as membership inference, which infers from a deployed model’s outputs whether a record was in its training set, show why privacy safeguards must be embedded in both model architecture and interface behavior.

↩︎ Membership inference, its mechanism, and why an empirical audit gives only a lower bound on leakage are established in Membership inference.

10 CCPA (California Consumer Privacy Act): CCPA grants California residents rights over covered personal information, including a deletion right subject to statutory exceptions (California Legislature 2023). For ML serving infrastructure, deletion requests create an architectural constraint that must be planned from the data pipeline forward, because raw-record deletion does not automatically remove a record’s influence from trained model parameters.

California Legislature. 2023. California Consumer Privacy Act of 2018. California Legislative Information.
Personal Information Protection Commission, Japan. 2023. Act on the Protection of Personal Information. Personal Information Protection Commission, Japan.

Legal frameworks make privacy an enforceable design concern. The GDPR, the California Consumer Privacy Act (CCPA),10 and Japan’s Act on the Protection of Personal Information establish different obligations and individual rights within their respective scopes (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2016; California Legislature 2023; Personal Information Protection Commission, Japan 2023). An implementation must identify which rules govern its collection, use, retention, and removal of data rather than treating consent or deletion as a uniform requirement across jurisdictions. That applicability decision belongs in the system’s documented data-governance requirements.

Privacy mechanisms limit specified data exposures under their threat models; they do not establish that a system’s decisions are safe. A model may satisfy its privacy requirements while still failing catastrophically on unexpected inputs, a complementary concern about how systems fail that section 16.8 takes up. Within privacy itself, the next decision is where the protected boundary sits in each deployment architecture.

16.6.2 Privacy across deployment tiers

Training a model with differential privacy does nothing to protect a user whose raw prompt is copied into a plaintext serving log at the API gateway. Keeping raw records on a phone, by contrast, protects against central database breaches at the cost of hiding fleet-wide telemetry from operators. Privacy inherits the same centralization trade-off across deployment tiers (privacy row of table 4). Aggregating data in the cloud enables high-capacity modeling and monitoring but concentrates exposure to breaches and surveillance, so it must be paired with strong encryption, access control, and auditability. Keeping data local on mobile, federated, or TinyML clients reduces that central exposure but changes the evidence available to fleet operators. Privacy controls must cover local storage, outputs, telemetry, and update channels; an endpoint with no update path needs controls that remain valid for its approved service life.

The constraint the table cannot show is that privacy claims must name their boundary. Differentially private training bounds training-data leakage through the released model; ordinary inference on fixed weights remains within that post-processing guarantee. It does not protect new private inputs copied into service logs or unrelated private-data accesses. Interface design, retention rules, rate limiting, and access control address those separate exposures. Opaque data collection can also violate user expectations, making consent surfaces and clear disclosure part of the privacy architecture.

Federated training draws three such boundaries (figure 7). Raw records stay on the client, secure aggregation hides individual updates only under its collusion and dropout assumptions, and central differential privacy bounds what the released model reveals only if the curator that adds the noise is trusted. Each protection covers a different quantity against a different observer, so a claim about one says nothing about the others. The privacy unit, clipping rule, protocol assumptions, and composition across rounds define each guarantee (Bonawitz et al. 2017; Dwork and Roth 2014).

Bonawitz, Keith, Vladimir Ivanov, Ben Kreuter, Antonio Marcedone, H. Brendan McMahan, Sarvar Patel, Daniel Ramage, Aaron Segal, and Karn Seth. 2017. “Practical Secure Aggregation for Privacy-Preserving Machine Learning.” Proceedings of the 2017 ACM SIGSAC Conference on Computer and Communications Security, 1175–91. https://doi.org/10.1145/3133956.3133982.
Dwork, Cynthia, and Aaron Roth. 2014. “The Algorithmic Foundations of Differential Privacy.” Foundations and Trends® in Theoretical Computer Science, Foundations and trends in theoretical computer science, vol. 9 (3-4): 211–487. https://doi.org/10.1561/0400000042.

An architecture decides where the privacy boundary sits; the model decides how much crosses it. A service can keep raw records behind every access control and still release training data through the weights it serves, which is where the governance of privacy guarantees begins.

16.6.3 Governing privacy guarantees

A language model that regurgitates a patient’s phone number from its training set or a classifier whose confidence scores reveal whether a specific customer was in the training cohort leaks private data through authorized outputs even when no database is breached. Governing a privacy guarantee starts from these output leaks, developed in Leakage through authorized outputs, and turns each into an audit obligation across data collection, model behavior, and user interaction. Because a membership-inference audit must state the attacker access it assumes and reads only as a lower bound on leakage, a formal bound has to come from the training mechanism.

For differential privacy, governance consumes the mechanism and accounting established in Differential Privacy. A differentially private stochastic gradient descent (DP-SGD) deployment must retain the clipping, noise, sampling, and composition configuration that supports its reported bound (Abadi et al. 2016). Reviewers then evaluate that bound alongside utility, subgroup performance, and the private-data accesses outside training. Naming the method is insufficient evidence for approving the complete service.

Abadi, M., A. Chu, I. Goodfellow, H. B. McMahan, I. Mironov, K. Talwar, and L. Zhang. 2016. “Deep Learning with Differential Privacy.” Proceedings of the 2016 ACM SIGSAC Conference on Computer and Communications Security, CCS ’16, 308–18. https://doi.org/10.1145/2976749.2978318.

Differential privacy introduces system-level trade-offs on the path where private data is accessed. Training noise can reduce utility, and attempts to recover it through additional data or optimization steps must be evaluated with the resulting privacy accounting and compute cost. If training occurs centrally, those costs do not automatically become extra work for an edge device performing ordinary inference on the released model. Local training or local randomization places different demands on the device. Data minimization, retention limits, and access controls remain separate requirements; feature obfuscation alone provides no differential privacy guarantee.

Systems Perspective 16.1: The price of privacy
Training a large language model on sensitive messages with DP-SGD reduces training-data extraction risk, but the protection is priced in compute and accuracy.

With the neighboring relation and \(\PrivacyAdditiveSlack\) fixed, a smaller \(\PrivacyBudget\) gives a stronger bound, and every change that lowers it (more noise, a smaller sampling rate, fewer steps) tends to cost accuracy or extra training. Differential Privacy derives these effects and lists the accountant inputs a reproducible DP-SGD result must record. Those recorded inputs, not the \(\PrivacyBudget\) value alone, are what a governance review retains. The three configurations below are planning assumptions whose \(\PrivacyBudget\) values an external accountant would have to confirm, as the CIFAR-10 accountant inputs of DP-SGD accountant inputs for image classification illustrate. Read them for the direction of each trade. The stronger configuration doubles the noise multiplier of the weaker one but also trains for more epochs, and more steps by themselves raise \(\PrivacyBudget\); only the accountant can confirm that the extra noise more than offsets the extra steps.

Illustrative stronger-privacy configuration (assumed accountant result \(\PrivacyBudget=\) 1): The scenario assumes per-example gradients are clipped to norm \(\GradientClippingNorm= 1.0\), 40 percent of updates are affected by clipping, and the noise multiplier is \(\PrivacyNoiseMultiplier= 1.0\). It further assumes 3× more epochs, a training-cost increase from a GPT-3-scale baseline of $4.6M to approximately $13.8M, and an accuracy drop of 6 percent.

Illustrative weaker-privacy configuration (assumed accountant result \(\PrivacyBudget=\) 8): The scenario assumes a noise multiplier of \(\PrivacyNoiseMultiplier= 0.5\), training overhead of 30 percent, and an accuracy drop of 1 percent.

Illustrative weakest configuration (assumed accountant result \(\PrivacyBudget=\) 10): The scenario assumes less noise and less than 1 percent accuracy loss, with a weaker formal privacy bound than the other two configurations under otherwise comparable accounting assumptions.

These scenarios compare formal privacy bounds, utility, and training cost; none of those quantities alone establishes user trust. Once a fixed model has been trained with differential privacy, ordinary inference is post-processing and does not consume additional training-data privacy budget. Additional privacy loss must be accounted for when a postdeployment mechanism makes new accesses to private data or releases additional differentially private statistics. The key engineering decision is to document and account for every private-data access and communicate the resulting trade-offs to users and regulators.

Released datasets need a boundary of their own. A \(\Glyph{k}\)-anonymous release (De-identification and k-anonymity) hides each record among at least \(\AnonymitySetSize\) that share its quasi-identifiers, a property of a specified release rather than of one transformed request. Differential privacy likewise requires a defined release mechanism, neighboring relation, and privacy accountant; noise injection alone does not establish a guarantee.

Privacy enforcement also depends on infrastructure beyond the model itself. Data collection interfaces must support informed consent and transparency. Logging systems must avoid retaining sensitive inputs unless strictly necessary, and must support access controls, expiration policies, and auditability. Model serving infrastructure must be designed to prevent overexposure of outputs that could leak internal model behavior or allow reconstruction of private data. These system-level mechanisms require close coordination between ML engineering, platform security, and organizational governance.

Privacy must be enforced throughout the machine learning lifecycle. Retraining pipelines must respect the approved deletion and data-use policies. Monitoring should collect only the identifying information justified by its purpose, with access and retention controls appropriate to that information. Privacy-aware telemetry collection and protected per-user audit trails can support investigation and remedy without making sensitive records broadly available in logs or dashboards.

The privacy assurance case therefore spans the whole pipeline. The formal privacy accountant covers the private-data accesses in its stated mechanism; interface controls, retention policies, and access records support separate claims about the surrounding service. Keeping these claims distinct makes gaps visible when a model, telemetry path, or data source changes.

Privacy preservation techniques create complex sociotechnical tensions that extend well beyond technical implementation. Differential privacy mechanisms may reduce model accuracy in ways that disproportionately affect underrepresented groups, creating conflicts between privacy and fairness objectives. These challenges require ongoing stakeholder engagement as detailed in section 16.9.4.3, where organizations must navigate competing values around data control, personalization, and regulatory compliance. Even a strong training-time privacy bound, however, treats the training set as fixed, leaving open what happens when a user revokes consent or demands erasure after the model is already in production.

16.6.4 Machine unlearning

Deleting a user’s row from a production database after an erasure request under GDPR or CCPA (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2016; California Legislature 2023) leaves their influence intact inside a 175-billion-parameter model trained on last month’s snapshot. Because privacy preservation does not end at training time, machine unlearning supplies a technical response when the approved deletion policy also requires addressing retained model state. The legal requirement and the evidence of removal must be specified separately, since retained influence alone does not establish that every deployment violates a deletion obligation. When the approved response does require removing training influence, the architecture must support the chosen method and its verification: an endpoint without sufficient data, training state, or compute needs a replacement model trained elsewhere, whereas a device with the required resources may perform the work locally. Placement follows those resource and state requirements rather than the deployment label alone.

Full retraining requires access to the permitted remaining training data and a defined training procedure. Three update strategies differ in what they recompute and what removal they guarantee (figure 8). Retraining from scratch on the retained data, the training set with the forget set \(\ForgetDataset\) removed, supplies a reference for exact removal relative to that procedure. Gradient ascent on the forget set increases its loss through updates of the form \(\ModelParameters + \LearningRate \nabla \LossFunction\), but increased loss alone does not demonstrate removal. Sharded, isolated, sliced, and aggregated (SISA) training partitions data into isolated shards \(\SisaTrainingShard_1, \SisaTrainingShard_2, \SisaTrainingShard_3\) whose constituent models are combined by aggregating their predictions at serving time. Within each shard, the data is further ordered into sequential slices introduced incrementally during training, with a checkpoint saved before each slice is added. A deletion therefore affects only the constituent model trained on the record’s shard, and rewinding that shard to the checkpoint saved just before the affected slice avoids replaying earlier slices.

Full retraining is a useful reference, but its feasibility depends on access to the permitted remaining data, the training procedure, and sufficient compute within the response deadline. A retention policy may remove data that retraining would need; a large training run may exceed the available budget. An alternative unlearning method must state its own data and state requirements rather than assume that avoiding full retraining also eliminates those dependencies.

Machine unlearning seeks to remove specified training influence without repeating the entire training run. Cao and Yang (2015) proposed converting suitable learning algorithms into a summation form, allowing a deletion to update the affected summaries and recompute the model. SISA instead localizes retraining to affected shards (Bourtoule et al. 2021). It still needs the retained data and training state required by that procedure. The removal claim must specify its reference and verification method; a loss increase on deleted examples is different evidence from retraining without them. Method selection therefore depends on retained state, model quality, operational cost, and the removal guarantee the deployment must support.

Cao, Yinzhi, and Junfeng Yang. 2015. “Towards Making Systems Forget with Machine Unlearning.” 2015 IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy, 463–80. https://doi.org/10.1109/sp.2015.35.
Bourtoule, Lucas, Varun Chandrasekaran, Christopher A. Choquette-Choo, Hengrui Jia, Adelin Travers, Baiwu Zhang, David Lie, and Nicolas Papernot. 2021. “Machine Unlearning.” 2021 IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy (SP), 141–59. https://doi.org/10.1109/sp40001.2021.00019.

For one isolated erasure, SISA cuts retraining cost about 100 times; a daily batch that touches most shards erodes the saving.

Napkin Math 16.6: The cost of forgetting
A user requests erasure of personal data under GDPR Article 17 (“Right to Erasure”). This scenario assumes that the approved response includes removing its influence from a model trained on 1 TB of data; that model-removal requirement is a scenario premise, not a conclusion derived from invoking the article.

Baseline (full retraining): In this sizing scenario, a 175B-parameter model at GPT-3 scale retrains on 1,024 NVIDIA A100 (80 GB SXM4) GPUs (dense FP16 \(\PeakRate =\) 312 TFLOP/s per accelerator at an assumed model FLOPs utilization (MFU) of about 32 percent) for approximately 36 days at a cost of roughly $4.6M.

Engineering fix: SISA training partitions data into \(\SisaShardCount=\) 100 independent shards, training 100 sub-models. For one datum confined to one shard, the example retrains that shard (1 percent of data). Assuming cost and elapsed time scale linearly with the data fraction at the same resource allocation gives $46,000 and approximately 8.6 hours. This simplified estimate omits slicing, checkpoint storage, validation, and deployment overhead; it is not a measured GPT-scale SISA result.

Trade-off: The scenario assumes an accuracy drop of 3–7 percent; the cost calculation does not establish that quality result. Serving must evaluate and aggregate 100 sub-models, with latency depending on parallelism and aggregation. Batch removal cost depends on how many of the 100 shards are touched. If the 1,000 requests/day arrive independently and uniformly across shards, a daily batch is likely to touch nearly all of them, eroding the sharding-only saving. Concentrated requests, batching policy, and saved slice states change the work. The operating comparison must therefore include the request distribution and response deadline, rather than the shard count alone.

Those cost and batching trade-offs explain why statutory erasure rights under GDPR, CCPA, and similar laws (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2016; California Legislature 2023) require context-dependent engineering triage rather than a universal command to retrain every model from scratch. Where model state itself retains personal data, as highlighted by incidents in which generative models reproduced personal content or copyrighted text, machine unlearning provides a technical strategy for reducing or removing a deleted record’s influence within a feasible compute budget.

From a systems perspective, machine unlearning introduces nontrivial architectural and operational requirements. Systems must be able to track data lineage, including which datapoints contributed to a given model version. This often requires structured metadata capture and training pipeline instrumentation. Additionally, systems must support user-facing deletion workflows, including authentication, submission, and feedback on deletion status. Verification may require maintaining versioned model registries, along with mechanisms for testing the stated removal guarantee against its reference training procedure. These operations must span data storage, training orchestration, model deployment, and auditing infrastructure, and they must be robust to failure or rollback.

Resource-constrained deployments amplify these challenges further. A TinyML device may lack the compute needed for retraining while still accepting a replacement model trained elsewhere. Unlearning then requires fleet inventory, authenticated updates, and evidence that affected versions have been replaced or retired. If no update path exists, operators must plan for physical servicing or withdrawal; initial data minimization reduces exposure but does not erase influence already present in deployed weights. Even in cloud-based systems, unlearning must coordinate model replicas, snapshots, and logs.

The need to remove training influence should be assessed during architecture and policy design, when teams can still plan the required lineage, retained state, and update paths. Retrofitting those capabilities after deployment may require reconstructing provenance or rebuilding training infrastructure. The assurance case should specify which removal obligations apply, how the chosen method will be evaluated, and how approved replacement models reach deployed instances.

Machine unlearning represents a shift in privacy thinking, from protecting what data is collected to controlling how long that data continues to affect system behavior. Exercise of any data right or challenge to an automated outcome, however, depends on whether users, clinicians, and auditors can see how the system works and why it produced a specific decision.

Self-Check: Question

  1. An engineering team implements Sharded, Isolated, Sliced, and Aggregated (SISA) training with \(\SisaShardCount = 100\) independent shards to support GDPR Article 17 (‘Right to Erasure’) for a 175B-parameter model. Under what operational workload does this sharded unlearning strategy lose its computational cost advantage over full model retraining?

    1. When the model is quantized from FP16 to INT8 for edge deployment
    2. When the daily volume of data erasure requests is high enough that requests touch essentially all \(\SisaShardCount\) shards in every retraining batch
    3. When the primary storage system transitions from NVMe SSDs to distributed object storage
    4. When the training pipeline uses Adam optimizer instead of stochastic gradient descent

  2. In Differentially Private Stochastic Gradient Descent (DP-SGD), what are the two core operations applied to gradients before weight updates, and what are their primary systems impacts?

    1. Gradient quantization to 4 bits and dropout pruning, reducing GPU memory by \(50\%\)
    2. Gradient checkpointing and layer freezing, eliminating backpropagation overhead
    3. Per-example gradient clipping to a maximum norm \(\GradientClippingNorm\) and calibrated Gaussian noise addition, whose privacy bound requires sampling and composition accounting and whose compute and utility costs depend on the configuration
    4. Spectral normalization of weight matrices and label smoothing, which eliminate adversarial vulnerability with zero compute overhead

  3. Why can deleting a user’s record from a primary database leave an incomplete ML deletion workflow, and what evidence must a machine-unlearning procedure provide?

  4. True or False: Once a neural network has been trained with DP-SGD under a privacy budget \(\PrivacyBudget = 1.0\), executing standard user inference queries in production consumes additional training-data privacy budget.

See Answers →

16.7 Explainability and Transparency

A rejected loan applicant, a clinician questioning a risk score, and an auditor reconstructing a contested decision each need a different account of the same model output, on a different deadline. Serving them requires disclosure about the system as a whole, explanation methods whose cost and fidelity fit the deployment, and an appeal path through which the affected person can act on what is disclosed.

16.7.1 The need for transparency

When ProPublica audited the COMPAS recidivism-risk score used in U.S. courts, it found that Black defendants were falsely flagged as future offenders at nearly twice the rate of white defendants (Angwin et al. 2016).11 Meanwhile, the vendor’s trade-secret claims prevented defendants, judges, and external investigators from inspecting the scoring formula or training provenance. Machine learning models often operate as opaque “black boxes,” producing outputs that users, developers, and regulators struggle to scrutinize. In high-stakes domains such as criminal justice, healthcare, and finance, that opacity blocks investigation and remediation even when external outcome audits expose a disparity.

11 COMPAS (Correctional Offender Management Profiling for Alternative Sanctions): ProPublica’s 2016 analysis found Black defendants were falsely flagged as future criminals at nearly twice the rate of white defendants (45 percent vs. 23 percent false positive rate) (Angwin et al. 2016). The proprietary, black-box nature of the system limited full inspection of model internals and training data, making the source of the observed error-rate disparity harder to investigate. Error-rate parity and predictive parity are distinct criteria, so the evaluation must name the criterion rather than treating one rate as a complete fairness verdict.

Angwin, Julia, Jeff Larson, Surya Mattu, and Lauren Kirchner. 2016. Machine Bias. ProPublica investigation.

The COMPAS case separates the two properties defined among the core principles. What investigators lacked was transparency, meaning disclosure of data sources, training procedures, evaluation protocols, and known limitations. Explanations of individual scores would have been a different kind of evidence, about particular decisions rather than the system. An attribution describes how a method assigns influence under its assumptions rather than supplying a causal account, a normative justification, or a path to recourse. Section 16.7.2 develops the methods, their costs, and their limits; the principle established here is that both properties are obligations the architecture must provision.

The importance of explainability and transparency extends beyond technical considerations to legal requirements. In many jurisdictions, these principles are legal obligations rather than optional guidance. Under the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR), solely automated decisions with legal or similarly significant effects trigger Article 22 safeguards and related transparency duties, including meaningful information about the logic involved under access and notice provisions, subject to statutory exceptions (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2016; European Data Protection Board 2018).12 Similar regulatory pressures in other domains reinforce the need to treat explainability and transparency as core architectural requirements.

12 GDPR (General Data Protection Regulation) Article 22: Article 22 gives data subjects protections against decisions based solely on automated processing that produce legal or similarly significant effects, with exceptions for contract necessity, authorization by law, or explicit consent plus safeguards (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2016). Related notice and access provisions, including Article 15, require meaningful information about the logic involved for qualifying automated decision-making, and EDPB guidance treats those duties as part of a broader profiling and automated-decision governance regime (European Data Protection Board 2018).

European Parliament, and Council of the European Union. 2016. Regulation (EU) 2016/679 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 27 April 2016. Official Journal of the European Union.
European Data Protection Board. 2018. Guidelines on Automated Individual Decision-Making and Profiling for the Purposes of Regulation 2016/679.

Implementing these principles requires anticipating the needs of different stakeholders, whose competing values and priorities are examined comprehensively in section 16.9.4.3. Designing for explainability and transparency therefore requires decisions about how and where to surface relevant information across the system lifecycle.

Transparency and explainability also support system reliability over time. As models are retrained or updated, mechanisms for interpretability and traceability allow detection of unexpected behavior, enable root cause analysis, and support governance. Embedded into the structure and operation of a system, these mechanisms provide the foundation for trust, oversight, and alignment with institutional and societal expectations.

While transparency and explainability enable stakeholders to understand system behavior, they do not guarantee that this behavior is equitable. A model can be fully transparent about how it makes decisions while still systematically disadvantaging certain groups, which is why the fairness criteria of section 16.5.1 remain a separate test. Fulfilling the explainability obligation itself requires choosing between models whose decision structure can be inspected directly and post-hoc methods that estimate feature influence after training.

16.7.2 Models of interpretability

A loan officer using a traditional rules-based system can identify the rule that produced a rejection: “The debt-to-income ratio exceeds 40 percent.” A neural network, however, produces a score through many interacting operations for which no single rule supplies the decision rationale. Explainability and interpretability provide bounded evidence about model behavior, such as feature influence, sensitivity, representative examples, or counterfactual changes. That evidence can support investigation and communication, but it becomes a justification only when the institution connects it to a valid decision policy, and it becomes recourse only when the user can take a feasible action that the system will honor.

Explanation evidence plays a role in system validation, error analysis, incident investigation, user communication, and some compliance workflows. In high-stakes domains such as healthcare, financial services, and autonomous decision systems, explanation methods can generate hypotheses about spurious correlations that engineers must test independently. For instance, a saliency13 or sensitivity analysis might indicate that a diagnostic model responds to image artifacts rather than medical features; controlled evaluation must then determine whether that dependence is causal and operationally significant. For qualifying automated decisions under GDPR-style access and notice provisions, systems may need to provide meaningful information about the logic involved, reinforcing the need for systematic support for explanation, documentation, and contestability.

13 Saliency maps: Gradient-based explanation that highlights which input regions most influenced a prediction by computing a backward pass, the same infrastructure used for training. Saliency methods are often cheaper than perturbation-heavy or subset-enumeration methods, making them attractive for edge and mobile deployments when the model supports gradients. The trade-off is that raw gradients can be noisy and may highlight input artifacts rather than meaningful features, so smoothing or repeated-gradient variants trade additional compute for more stable visualizations.

Two scopes of explanation place different demands on the system. Local explanations characterize influences or sensitivities around an individual prediction, so their cost depends on which decisions are explained and whether results can be reused. Global explanations characterize broader model behavior, using model structure, representative inputs, or aggregated decision evidence. They may be computed offline or updated as new evidence arrives. Scope describes what the explanation covers; the method and delivery requirement determine where and when it runs.

Explainability methods can be broadly categorized based on when they operate and how they relate to model structure. Post-hoc methods are applied after training, but they are not necessarily black-box methods. Model-agnostic approaches such as LIME (Ribeiro et al. 2016) and KernelSHAP can operate through model queries, whereas input gradients, integrated gradients14 (Sundararajan et al. 2017), and gradient-weighted class activation mapping (GradCAM)15 (Selvaraju et al. 2017) require differentiable-model access to gradients or internal activations. Other SHAP implementations (Lundberg and Lee 2017) may exploit model structure.

Ribeiro, Marco Tulio, Sameer Singh, and Carlos Guestrin. 2016. “Why Should I Trust You?: Explaining the Predictions of Any Classifier.” Proceedings of the 22nd ACM SIGKDD International Conference on Knowledge Discovery and Data Mining, 1135–44. https://doi.org/10.1145/2939672.2939778.

14 Integrated gradients: An attribution method that integrates gradients along the straight path from a baseline input to the actual input. Sensitivity requires a nonzero attribution when a single changed feature accounts for a changed prediction; implementation invariance requires identical attributions for functionally equivalent models (Sundararajan et al. 2017). The authors argued that most earlier attribution methods violate at least one of these axioms. These properties concern the exact method, while numerical integration introduces an approximation whose accuracy depends on the sampling of the path. The deployment must therefore budget repeated gradient evaluations and check approximation error rather than treating a fixed step count as sufficient for every model and input.

Sundararajan, Mukund, Ankur Taly, and Qiqi Yan. 2017. “Axiomatic Attribution for Deep Networks.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 70: 3319–28.

15 GradCAM (gradient-weighted class activation mapping): Selvaraju et al. (2017) generalized Class Activation Mapping to any convolutional neural network (CNN) architecture by using gradients flowing into the final convolutional layer to produce spatial importance maps. Because it reuses gradient computation, GradCAM can be far cheaper than perturbation-heavy explanation methods, but whether it fits real-time medical imaging or autonomous-vehicle pipelines depends on the model, hardware, batch size, and explanation-frequency budget.

Selvaraju, R. R., M. Cogswell, A. Das, R. Vedantam, D. Parikh, and D. Batra. 2017. “Grad-CAM: Visual Explanations from Deep Networks via Gradient-Based Localization.” 2017 IEEE International Conference on Computer Vision (ICCV), 618–26. https://doi.org/10.1109/iccv.2017.74.

16 Shapley values: From cooperative game theory, Shapley values fairly distribute a payoff among players based on marginal contributions across all possible orderings. In ML explainability, features are “players” and the prediction is the “payoff.” The mathematical guarantees (efficiency, symmetry, null player) make SHAP a widely used attribution framework with clear axiomatic appeal, but exact subset enumeration grows as \(2^{\AttributionFeatureCount}\) for \(\AttributionFeatureCount\) features, which is why production systems often rely on model-specific algorithms, sampling approximations, caching, or asynchronous explanation paths (Lundberg and Lee 2017).

Lundberg, Scott M., and Su-In Lee. 2017. “A Unified Approach to Interpreting Model Predictions.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 30: 4765–74.

Post-hoc approaches are widely used in image and tabular domains, where explanations can be rendered as saliency maps or feature rankings. To illustrate how SHAP attribution works in practice, consider a trained random forest model predicting loan approval (\(\text{approve}=1\), \(\text{deny}=0\)) based on three features: income, debt_ratio, and credit_score. For a specific applicant who was denied, with income of $45,000, debt ratio of 0.55 (55 percent of income goes to debt), and credit score of 620, the model outputs \(\Pr(\text{approve}) = 0.08\), that is, denial with probability 0.92. SHAP values, based on Shapley values from cooperative game theory,16 measure each feature’s contribution to moving the approval probability from a baseline (the expected prediction over a declared background distribution, here \(\Pr(\text{approve}) = 0.50\)) to this individual prediction.

The contribution of a feature depends on which other features are already known, so adding income first and adding it last can shift the prediction by different amounts. SHAP resolves that ambiguity by averaging a feature’s marginal contribution over every order in which the features could be added, under a specified value function. In this illustrative decomposition the averaged contributions are \(-0.05\) for income, \(-0.25\) for debt_ratio, and \(-0.12\) for credit_score.

Shapley values are additive, so the contributions sum from the baseline to the prediction: \(0.50 - 0.05 - 0.25 - 0.12 = 0.08 = \Pr(\text{approve})\), which is \(\Pr(\text{deny}) = 0.92\). Under this attribution setup, debt ratio has the largest assigned contribution to the output, followed by credit score and income. This ranking is a hypothesis about model sensitivity under the chosen background distribution, not evidence that debt ratio caused the decision or that lowering it below 40 percent would flip the output. A recourse claim requires a separately validated counterfactual that respects feature dependencies, immutable attributes, feasible user actions, and the decision policy that will apply when the user returns.

Exact subset enumeration grows exponentially; model-specific algorithms can avoid it.

The computational cost depends on the attribution algorithm. Enumerating all subsets would evaluate \(2^3=8\) coalitions in this example and \(2^{20}\approx1\) million for twenty features. A random forest need not use enumeration: TreeSHAP exploits tree structure to compute exact attributions under its supported value-function assumptions. KernelSHAP estimates contributions from sampled coalitions, while DeepSHAP propagates approximations through network components. Gradient-based attribution can need fewer model evaluations still, but only where the deployed runtime supports gradient computation, and lower cost does not establish explanation fidelity. Explanation capacity must therefore be budgeted for the chosen method rather than inferred from feature count alone.

Another post-hoc approach involves counterfactual explanations, which estimate how a model output changes under specified input modifications. These are relevant for decision-facing applications such as credit or hiring systems, but a mathematical output flip is not automatically recourse. A useful counterfactual must distinguish mutable from immutable features, preserve domain constraints and feature dependencies, identify an action within the user’s control, and remain valid under the institution’s future decision policy (Wachter et al. 2017). Counterfactual generation and validation therefore require domain models, policy versioning, and operational commitments in addition to constrained optimization.

Wachter, Sandra, Brent Mittelstadt, and Chris Russell. 2017. “Counterfactual Explanations Without Opening the Black Box: Automated Decisions and the GDPR.” SSRN Electronic Journal 31: 841. https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.3063289.
Kim, B., M. Wattenberg, J. Gilmer, C. Cai, J. Wexler, F. Viegas, and R. Sayres. 2018. “Interpretability Beyond Feature Attribution: Quantitative Testing with Concept Activation Vectors (TCAV).” Proceedings of the 35th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML) 80: 2668–77.

A third class of techniques relies on concept-based explanations, which attempt to align learned model features with human-interpretable concepts. For example, testing with concept activation vectors (TCAV) represents user-defined concepts as vectors in activation space and tests how sensitive a model’s predictions are to those concepts in its internal representation (Kim et al. 2018). These methods are especially useful in domains where subject matter experts expect explanations in familiar semantic terms. However, they require training data with concept annotations or auxiliary models for concept detection, which introduces additional infrastructure dependencies.

Post-hoc methods answer method-specific questions about model behavior. Even an exact attribution under a chosen value function does not recover a causal rationale or justify the decision policy. Approximate methods add estimation or surrogate error to those interpretive limits. Evaluation must therefore establish both what the method computes and whether that evidence serves the intended debugging, communication, or review task.

Inherently interpretable models expose a decision structure that the intended audience can inspect. Small decision trees, short rule lists, and sparse linear models with meaningful features can support this goal; a large tree or high-dimensional linear model may not. Nearest-neighbor comparisons likewise require an understandable similarity metric and representative examples. Interpretability therefore depends on structure, features, and audience, not merely the model-family label. In high-stakes applications, model selection should evaluate whether a sufficiently accurate, inspectable model meets the task before assuming that post-hoc explanations are necessary (Rudin 2019).

Rudin, C. 2019. “Stop Explaining Black Box Machine Learning Models for High Stakes Decisions and Use Interpretable Models Instead.” Nature Machine Intelligence 1 (5): 206–15. https://doi.org/10.1038/s42256-019-0048-x.

Six common model families fall along an interpretability continuum in three groups (figure 9). Sparse linear models (explained via coefficients) and small decision trees (via split paths) are interpretable by design. Prototype and concept models are hybrid architectures explained via exemplars. Tree ensembles (via TreeSHAP), deep networks (via gradients and SHAP), and foundation models (via probing) require post-hoc explanation. While this ordering provides a structural heuristic across model families, dimensionality and scale can shift boundaries; a million-node decision tree or dense linear regression can prove just as opaque as a deep neural network. This distinction is central to choosing an appropriate model for a given application, particularly in settings where regulatory scrutiny or stakeholder trust is essential.

Hybrid approaches aim to combine the representational capacity of deep models with the transparency of interpretable components. Concept bottleneck models (Koh et al. 2020), for example, first predict intermediate, interpretable variables and then use a simple classifier to produce the final prediction. ProtoPNet models (Chen et al. 2019) classify examples by comparing them to learned prototypes, offering visual analogies for users to understand predictions. These hybrid methods are attractive in domains that demand partial transparency, but they introduce new system design considerations, such as the need to store and index learned prototypes and surface them at inference time.

Koh, Pang Wei, Thao Nguyen, Yew Siang Tang, Stephen Mussmann, Emma Pierson, Been Kim, and Percy Liang. 2020. “Concept Bottleneck Models.” Proceedings of the 37th International Conference on Machine Learning, ICML 2020, 13-18 July 2020, Virtual Event, Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 119: 5338–48.
Chen, Chaofan, Oscar Li, Daniel Tao, Alina Barnett, Cynthia Rudin, and Jonathan Su. 2019. “This Looks Like That: Deep Learning for Interpretable Image Recognition.” In Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 32: Annual Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems 2019, NeurIPS 2019, December 8-14, 2019, Vancouver, BC, Canada, edited by Hanna M. Wallach, Hugo Larochelle, Alina Beygelzimer, Florence d’Alché-Buc, Emily B. Fox, and Roman Garnett. Curran Associates.
Olah, C., N. Cammarata, L. Schubert, G. Goh, M. Petrov, and S. Carter. 2020. “Zoom in: An Introduction to Circuits.” Distill 5 (3): e00024–001. https://doi.org/10.23915/distill.00024.001.
Geiger, Atticus, Hanson Lu, Thomas Icard, and Christopher Potts. 2021. “Causal Abstractions of Neural Networks.” In Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 34: Annual Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems 2021, NeurIPS 2021, December 6-14, 2021, Virtual, edited by Marc’Aurelio Ranzato, Alina Beygelzimer, Yann N. Dauphin, Percy Liang, and Jennifer Wortman Vaughan. Curran Associates.

Work such as Olah et al. (2020) and Geiger et al. (2021) applies mechanistic interpretability to reverse-engineer the internal operations of neural networks. This line of work, inspired by program analysis and neuroscience, attempts to map neurons, layers, or activation patterns to specific computational functions. Claims about such mechanisms require instrumentation and tests that support the proposed interpretation. These research methods can contribute evidence about internal computation, but do not establish a complete audit of every deployed model.

From a systems perspective, explainability introduces a number of architectural dependencies. Explanations must be generated, stored, surfaced, and evaluated within system constraints.
