The required infrastructure may include explanation APIs, memory for storing attribution maps, visualization libraries, and logging mechanisms that capture intermediate model behavior. Models must often be instrumented with hooks or configured to support repeated evaluations, particularly for explanation methods that require sampling, perturbation, or backpropagation.

The sociotechnical challenges of explainability center on the gap between technical explanations and human understanding. While algorithms can generate feature attributions and gradient maps, stakeholders often need explanations that align with their mental models, domain expertise, and decision-making processes. A radiologist reviewing an AI-generated diagnosis needs explanations that reference medical concepts and visual patterns, not abstract neural network activations. This translation challenge requires ongoing collaboration between technical teams and domain experts to develop explanation formats that are both technically accurate and practically meaningful. Explanations can shape human decision-making in unexpected ways, creating new responsibilities for how explanatory information is presented and interpreted. That gap between a technically valid explanation and a usable one is the first instance of a broader problem. Every control in this chapter operates inside human workflows and institutions that no model-level method governs. Where explanation work runs is fixed by the audience, the question it must answer, and the deadline for answering it.

16.7.3 Explanation placement

Bridging that gap under production deadlines starts with where the explanation computation can afford to run. Running sampled KernelSHAP inline on a microcontroller or a 10 ms control loop will miss the actuation deadline before a single attribution finishes, whereas deferring every attribution to an offline batch queue leaves a rejected applicant with no immediate reason for the outcome. Computational capacity, latency budget, interface design, and data access constrain both the explanation method and its placement. A cloud worker can execute expensive SHAP or LIME attribution; a constrained endpoint may use a cheaper gradient-based saliency map if its runtime supports gradients. Devices unable to generate explanations locally may preserve bounded decision records for later analysis by a backend or during servicing, the per-tier split that the explainability row of table 4 records.

Audience and timing further constrain explanation design. An end user may need a concise summary (“elevated heart rate during sleep”), while a developer or auditor may need attribution maps, concept activations, or decision traces. The required evidence depends on what each person must understand or do. A drone or industrial controller must keep explanation work within its control deadline or move that work off the critical path. Financial and clinical workflows likewise need a specified delivery deadline, which may allow analysis after the automated decision but before review or appeal. Planning for the audience and deadline, within the deployment’s resource limits, makes interpretability a design constraint.

Storage requirements also scale significantly with explanation needs. Storing SHAP values for tabular data requires approximately 4–8 bytes per feature per prediction, so monthly storage is 30 million predictions times the feature count times 4–8 bytes for a system logging 1 million predictions daily. Gradient attribution maps for images can require 1–10 MB per explanation depending on resolution; logging every image explanation at the same 1 million-per-day rate would require roughly 30–300 TB per month unless explanations are sampled, compressed, or retained for a shorter window. These volumes require careful data lifecycle management and retention policies.

Compression and optimization techniques also affect explainability. Pruning, quantization, and architectural simplifications often used in TinyML or mobile settings can distort internal representations or disable gradient flow, degrading the reliability of attribution-based explanations. In such cases, interpretability must be validated postoptimization to ensure that it remains meaningful and trustworthy. Even an explanation that survives optimization and fits its latency and storage budget, however, protects the user only if the surrounding service provides a way to act on it.

16.7.4 Transparency and contestability

Showing a rejected loan applicant a feature-attribution chart accomplishes nothing if the app offers no button to dispute an error in the credit file, no human reviewer with authority to overturn the rejection, and no immutable snapshot of the inputs that produced the score. Explanations serve accountability only when stakeholders can both understand and challenge system decisions. Transparency allows users, developers, auditors, and regulators to inspect how a system functions, assess its limitations, and identify sources of harm, but disclosure alone is not enough. In high-stakes domains, individuals and institutions need contestability: the operational ability to interrogate, appeal, and reverse an automated decision.

Documentation tools such as model cards and datasheets for datasets formalize system-level disclosure by recording how models are trained, what data they rely on, what assumptions are embedded in their design, and what known limitations affect their use. Those artifacts improve governance, support compliance, and inform user expectations, yet static documentation still leaves affected individuals without interface tools, procedural access, or institutional support when a specific decision harms them.

To move from transparency to contestability, machine learning systems must integrate mechanisms for explanation, recourse, and feedback. Explanation provides understandable reasons for system outputs, tailored to the operational context and needs of the person receiving them. Recourse identifies feasible changes through which individuals could receive a different outcome under the stated decision policy. A proposed change needs to be actionable for that person; a counterfactual prediction alone does not guarantee an outcome after the model or policy changes. Feedback enables users and domain experts to dispute outcomes, report errors, or signal concerns, routing those signals into oversight processes and model updates.

These mechanisms can be missing from the user-facing workflow even when internal documentation exists. A mobile loan application might display a rejection without explaining how to submit additional information or reach an appeal reviewer. An external appeal process can still exist, but users need a discoverable route to it and access to the evidence relevant to their case. Similarly, a clinical model may produce a risk score without giving the physician enough context to question its use for a particular patient. If subgroup errors are neither observable nor contestable, the resulting harm becomes harder to diagnose and correct.

From a systems perspective, enabling contestability requires coordination across technical and institutional components. Models must expose sufficient information to support explanation. Interfaces must surface that information in a usable and timely way, so that organizational processes can review feedback, respond to appeals, and update system behavior. Logging and auditing infrastructure must also track user interventions and override decisions alongside model outputs. Human overrides and decision abstention can support this process only when qualified reviewers have capacity, evidence, and authority to resolve the deferred cases within the required deadline.

Implementing contestability imposes concrete infrastructure costs that scale with system throughput and complexity. Storing the necessary metadata to reconstruct a decision (input features, model version, and decision thresholds) requires persistent storage whose footprint depends on feature dimensionality, explanation payloads, and retention windows. Generating on-demand explanations using Shapley values or counterfactuals can add enough latency that contested decisions often need asynchronous processing queues to preserve serving service level agreements (SLAs). Meeting record-keeping and automatic-logging obligations for high-risk systems under frameworks such as the EU AI Act requires storage, provenance, retention controls, and oversight capacity to be budgeted as part of the inference fleet rather than as a separate policy artifact (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2024).

War Story 16.1: Apple Card: A systems reconstruction of contested decisions (2019)
Context: In 2019, Apple and Goldman Sachs faced intense public scrutiny after a customer reported a credit limit 20\(\times\) that offered to his wife despite joint tax filings. Other customers raised similar concerns about unequal terms (New York State Department of Financial Services 2021).

Evidence boundary: The public record documents complaints, the investigation, the regulator’s finding, and changes to appeal policy. It does not disclose the underwriting pipeline’s latency target or internal explanation architecture. The systems account below is therefore an explicit reconstruction, not a report of Apple or Goldman Sachs implementation details.

Reconstructed mechanism: A low-latency underwriting service that returns only a limit and generic adverse-action reason leaves support staff unable to inspect the decision snapshot, distinguish data errors from policy effects, or route a contested outcome to a reviewer. The missing interface is broader than feature attribution. It includes versioned input provenance, policy and model versions, reasons appropriate to the decision process, appeal state, and authority to correct an error.

Observed impact: The New York Department of Financial Services found no violation of fair-lending law in its investigation, but the complaints still exposed a trust and contestability failure. Customers and frontline staff lacked a satisfactory account of how apparently inconsistent outcomes could be reviewed (New York State Department of Financial Services 2021).

Systems lesson: High-stakes decision systems need a contestability interface, not merely an attribution API. A defensible architecture preserves the decision-time evidence needed for audit, generates appropriate reasons without claiming that attribution is causation, routes appeals to an authorized human, records remediation, and tests the full workflow under its latency and retention constraints.

New York State Department of Financial Services. 2021. Report on Apple Card Investigation. New York State Department of Financial Services.

The Apple Card reconstruction names the pieces such a system lacks. One architectural pattern that supplies them is a contestability stack, analogous to distributed tracing in microservices, that coordinates four coupled stages across the operational pipeline. Decision provenance preserves a verifiable link from each output back to its governing policy, model version, and input snapshot, protected by authenticated logs or cryptographic integrity checks. Explanation generation budgets explanation methods tailored for appeals, audits, and routine communication, offloading compute-intensive methods to asynchronous workers while keeping lightweight techniques on the request path. When decisions are challenged, appeal routing directs contested cases to qualified human reviewers equipped with domain context. Closing the loop, outcome tracking records whether the appeal upheld or overturned the machine decision, feeding empirical discrepancies back into evaluation and retraining. Missing decision-time records can prevent investigators from reconstructing a contested decision, even if aggregate debugging remains possible; the required records follow the investigation and remedy contract.

Provenance anchors every appeal; tracked outcomes loop back into retraining.

The stack need not run where the decision runs. An edge or TinyML endpoint can hand its decision records to a backend review process at the next synchronization or servicing visit, which lengthens the appeal’s evidence path without removing the obligation to offer one. In every tier, transparency supports accountability only when users and institutions can use the disclosed information to question and correct automated decisions. Contestability and appeals, however, operate after a decision has been made; when a system acts in safety-critical environments or generates open-ended outputs at scale, governance must also bound how the model can fail before harm occurs.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A model-agnostic explanation service enumerates all feature coalitions for 20 input features, taking at least one microsecond per coalition. Why does this exhaustive Shapley calculation exceed a 100 ms inline serving budget?

    1. It evaluates \(2^{20}\) coalitions, requiring more than one second even at one microsecond per coalition; a cheaper method or separate explanation path is needed.
    2. Because Shapley values can only be computed using double-precision arithmetic that crashes GPU tensor cores
    3. Because cooperative game theory axioms prohibit computing feature contributions on tabular data
    4. Because exact SHAP requires accessing the client’s raw hardware registers during inference

  2. Explain the two fundamental axioms satisfied by Integrated Gradients (Sensitivity and Implementation Invariance) and why they make Integrated Gradients superior to vanilla input gradients.

  3. Why do local and global explanations place different demands on explanation infrastructure?

    1. Local explanations characterize an individual prediction; global explanations characterize broader model behavior using model structure, representative inputs, or aggregated evidence. Their methods and delivery deadlines determine execution and capacity needs.
    2. Local explanations require no model access, whereas global explanations require retraining the model on every request.
    3. Global explanations must run inline on every request, whereas local explanations can only be generated during training.
    4. Local and global explanations have identical cost because both are computed from the same Shapley enumeration.

  4. A counterfactual explanation shows that a denied loan applicant would have been approved with a debt ratio below 40 percent. Explain why this output flip does not by itself give the applicant recourse.

  5. True or False: Any decision tree or linear model is inherently interpretable, so choosing one of those model families removes the need for explanation evidence.

  6. For an appeal workflow that retrieves decision evidence before explanation, then sends both to a reviewer and records the outcome, order these actions: (1) Route the case and explanation to an authorized credit reviewer, (2) Retrieve the decision record and governing model and policy versions, (3) Record the review outcome and propagate any authorized remedy, (4) Generate explanation evidence, checking any proposed recourse for feasibility and policy validity.

See Answers →

16.8 AI Safety, Robustness, and Generative Controls

A model that withstands every tested perturbation can still cause harm when the operating environment leaves its validated envelope or when its objective rewards the wrong behavior. Safety governance therefore spans robustness claims bounded by a threat model, control surfaces for generative systems whose outputs cannot be enumerated in advance, and the alignment of the objective itself as systems act with greater autonomy.

16.8.1 Safety and robustness

A perception model that passes every \(\ell_\infty\) adversarial benchmark in the lab can still contribute to a fatal crash if the vehicle’s motion planner suppresses emergency braking or waits too long to alert a distracted safety driver. Safety and robustness connect technical failure behavior to its consequences for people. Robust AI owns the attack models, robustness evaluations, adversarial training, certification, corruption testing, drift detection, and abstention mechanisms; governance consumes those mechanisms through an evidence interface. Evidence that a model tolerates specified hardware faults, adversarial attacks, or distribution shifts supports a bounded robustness claim. It does not establish that every deployment is safe or that the objective serves users’ interests. A model may tolerate the tested bit flips and perturbations yet fail under untested conditions, or reliably optimize an objective misaligned with user welfare.

↩︎ The three robustness pillars, environmental shifts, input-level attacks, and system-level faults such as bit flips, are established in The three pillars of robust AI.

The responsible-AI question is therefore not which attack or defense to implement, but whether the robustness claim has a bounded operating envelope, evidence appropriate to the threat model, an accountable owner, and a rule that invalidates approval. An evaluation may bound errors under a specified input variation without establishing that the surrounding workflow is safe. Automated-driving failures show why the envelope must cover more than the model, because they can combine perception, prediction, safety-driver, and organizational-control problems rather than reduce to a single model error; section 16.8.4 examines one such crash.

A robustness claim should specify the protected task and population, attacker knowledge and capabilities, perturbation or corruption set, evaluation distribution, model and preprocessing versions, quality and latency constraints, fallback behavior, and known exclusions. Adversarial inputs (Adversarial Attacks) and covariate, label, and concept shift (Distribution shift and concept drift) are the failure sources such a claim most often has to bound. Empirical attack results show observed failures under a test protocol; they do not establish robustness against adaptive or untested attacks. A certificate proves a property only for its stated norm, radius, confidence, and model configuration; it does not establish safety for the end-to-end workflow. Governance records those boundaries so a narrow result cannot be promoted into a general safety assertion.

The latency budget constrains which safety checks can run before an action. A cloud service may combine uncertainty estimators, distributional-change detectors, adversarial input filtering, and API rate limiting. A control loop with a millisecond budget needs bounded local checks and fallback actions that meet its deadline, with heavier analysis deferred to fleet services, the division the safety row of table 4 records by tier. Even a disconnected TinyML endpoint can execute input-range checks or watchdog logic; the absence of a remote update channel instead limits how operators change those controls after deployment.

Fallback is itself a latency-budget problem. A delivery robot that abstains17 when pedestrian-detection confidence is low still needs a validated response before the physical safety deadline. A bounded local maneuver may place it in a safe state while a human reviewer resolves the case on a longer timescale. The response and review paths therefore need separate deadlines and capacity budgets. Whether the system can fail visibly, controllably, and safely depends on sensing, confidence estimation, fallback routing, and recovery. Those choices also define who can observe harm, who can intervene, and who remains accountable for the outcome.

17 Abstention: The practice of refusing predictions when confidence falls below a threshold. Abstention can reduce high-risk errors, but only by shifting some cases to fallback infrastructure such as human reviewers, rule-based defaults, or escalation queues. A driving system must select a validated fallback, such as a minimal-risk maneuver; handing control to a driver is viable only if a driver is present, ready, and has sufficient response time. Medical AI may route ambiguous cases to specialist review, subject to its own queue-capacity and response-time budget.

↩︎ The per-request competence contract, which combines calibration and out-of-distribution scores into a selective-prediction policy to answer, abstain, or escalate, is established in Per-request competence and selective prediction.

The evidence bundle must also connect model behavior to system action. It identifies who owns the robustness claim, which team can stop serving, how abstentions and low-confidence cases are routed, which telemetry detects departure from the approved envelope, and how an incident reaches affected users and downstream operators. A fallback must meet the relevant safety deadline. A monitoring signal becomes an operational safeguard only when it reaches an authorized response path, whether that response is local containment, restricted use, or a validated rollback.

Approval expires when the threat model, model, preprocessing, sensor, data distribution, deployment environment, or fallback path changes materially. The invalidation rule should state which change triggers a full red-team evaluation, which permits a narrower regression suite, and which requires restricted operation while evidence is rebuilt. This ownership-and-invalidation contract is the governance contribution, turning those robustness mechanisms into a reviewable claim that remains bounded over time.

16.8.2 Generative control surfaces

A binary loan classifier has two possible outputs to audit. A large language model (LLM) such as Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3), by contrast, can be steered by a user prompt or a poisoned retrieved document into generating defamatory claims, leaking personal data, or refusing benign requests from one dialect more often than another. Generative AI does not replace core fairness, privacy, and explainability concerns; it changes the control surface. Instead of only auditing labels or feature attributions, operators must govern prompts, retrieved context, reward models, and open-ended outputs. Fairness remains concerned with how quality, refusals, errors, representation, and access are distributed across people and groups, but open-ended outputs make those quantities harder to define and sample. Alignment introduces a separate problem, which is keeping stochastic outputs and actions consistent with intended objectives and policies across a combinatorial range of prompts. The two questions differ in kind. Fairness asks how benefits, errors, and burdens are distributed among affected people; alignment asks whether an optimizing system pursues the objective its designers and legitimate stakeholders intended. An aligned system can implement an unfair objective faithfully, and a statistically fair classifier can still optimize the wrong social goal, so production systems need both fairness evaluation and behavioral alignment rather than relabeling one as the other.

A common multi-stage alignment pipeline uses reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF),18 running in two coupled lanes to bridge human preferences and model weights (figure 10). An upper execution path advances the policy from pretraining through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to proximal policy optimization (PPO), and a lower supervision path uses human preference comparisons to train a reward model \(\RewardModel(\ModelInput, \GeneratedResponse)\). The reward signal drives PPO updates, while the frozen SFT checkpoint supplies a reference for a Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence penalty. The penalty discourages departure from that reference; it does not impose a hard bound on every response or establish policy compliance.

18 RLHF (reinforcement learning from human feedback): Christiano et al. (2017) demonstrated deep reinforcement learning from human preferences by training agents from non-expert comparisons between trajectory segments. In the LLM setting, Ouyang et al. (2022) adapted this pipeline for InstructGPT: supervised fine-tuning from demonstrations, reward-model training from ranked model outputs, and reinforcement-learning fine-tuning against the learned reward model. The systems cost is an additional human-feedback data pipeline, reward-model training and evaluation, and governance over rater instructions; the representativeness of the rater pool and labeling policy shapes whose preferences the model internalizes.

Christiano, P. F., J. Leike, T. B. Brown, M. Martic, S. Legg, and D. Amodei. 2017. “Deep Reinforcement Learning from Human Preferences.” In Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 30: Annual Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems 2017, December 4-9, 2017, Long Beach, CA, USA, edited by Isabelle Guyon, Ulrike von Luxburg, Samy Bengio, Hanna M. Wallach, Rob Fergus, S. V. N. Vishwanathan, and Roman Garnett. Curran Associates.
Ouyang, L., J. Wu, X. Jiang, D. Almeida, C. L. Wainwright, P. Mishkin, C. Zhang, et al. 2022. “Training Language Models to Follow Instructions with Human Feedback.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 27730–44. https://doi.org/10.52202/068431-2011.

Collecting the human comparisons that train the reward model in figure 10 carries a direct data-acquisition cost. In this scenario, 50,000 to 500,000 pairwise comparisons at $0.50 to $5 per label put the labeling budget between $25,000 and $2.5M, a hundredfold spread set by how many judgments the organization buys and how expert its raters must be. Updating the policy with PPO against those learned rewards can also create an alignment tax, a regression in some capability metrics when optimization shifts toward preference or safety objectives; its direction and magnitude depend on the model, data, objectives, and evaluation suite rather than following a universal percentage. The reliance on human raters introduces a representativeness gap as well. If the labeling budget buys judgments from only a narrow demographic slice, the resulting model may encode that pool’s preferences while performing differently for other populations. Constitutional AI offers an alternative engineering path, using explicit principles to guide AI feedback on outputs (Bai et al. 2022), which changes rather than eliminates the questions of whose values are represented and how they are governed.

Bai, Yuntao et al. 2022. Constitutional AI: Harmlessness from AI Feedback.

↩︎ The four-model coordination that a PPO-style RLHF step imposes on distributed training is established in The multi-model coordination problem.

In retrieval-augmented generation architectures, responsibility extends beyond the core model. An LLM may pass alignment evaluations yet generate harmful responses when retrieval supplies context outside those evaluations. A retrieval index that disproportionately surfaces biased historical documents can influence the resulting answers despite safety training. Context filtering is one possible control, alongside source selection, retrieval evaluation, and output checks. Its effectiveness must be tested on the intended use; a toxicity filter does not establish that retrieved material or generated answers are unbiased.

In many LLM serving stacks, the system prompt operates as an early configuration control alongside retrieval filtering, policy classifiers, telemetry, and rollout gates. These instructions (for example, “You are a helpful assistant. Do not provide medical advice.”) express intended behavior; they do not independently enforce authorization or prevent every prohibited output. When one model serves many products, each with its own system prompt, managing the prompts becomes a distributed configuration-management problem akin to weight distribution. An unversioned prompt change can alter behavior across many interactions, so prompt version control, alignment regression testing, and gradual rollouts belong in the release process.

Together, system prompts and RLHF alignments act as important, yet fragile, technical guardrails. Jailbreaks and other edge cases can violate the policy learned during training; the reward model is not necessarily a runtime filter. Safety therefore requires evaluating the deployed control system as well as its training objective. Each of these guardrails, like the privacy and robustness controls before it, is credible only with evidence that it still holds in the system that actually runs.

↩︎ Jailbreaks, which bypass alignment controls, and prompt injection, which crosses the boundary between instructions and data, are established in LLM-specific attack vectors.

16.8.3 AI safety and value alignment

Prompt filters and reward-model training still assume that the optimization target itself captures what stakeholders want, an assumption that breaks when an autonomous trading engine executes thousands of orders per second or a recommendation ranker reshapes billions of feeds faster than any human can review individual decisions. At that operating speed, the governing question is no longer only whether individual outputs are fair, private, or explainable, but whether the objective the system optimizes still serves the people it acts on, and what bounds its actions before review. Human launch authority remains necessary, but continuous human intervention may be unavailable. AI safety examines how systems can optimize objectives misaligned with human intentions, operate in unpredictable environments, or pursue goals through methods their designers never anticipated, and which controls can bound those actions.

The controls of the preceding sections retain their roles, but none governs autonomous action by itself. Bias detection (section 16.5.2.1) yields metrics rather than a remediation policy. A perception system that under-detects pedestrians with mobility aids needs an operating envelope that connects the metric to bounded runtime responses and to fleet-level investigation or suspension. Explanation (section 16.7.2) needs an audience and an action path; SHAP attributions from an autonomous trading system cannot influence a decision if no control consumes them before thousands of trades execute. Differential privacy (section 16.6.3) bounds leakage about a protected unit without determining whether the objective serves that person’s welfare, so a recommender can satisfy a local differential privacy guarantee while optimizing an engagement metric that rewards harmful content. Each method needs a defined connection to the runtime controls and human authority that can respond to its findings.

Alignment work emphasizes keeping a system’s pursued objectives consistent with human intentions over time (Russell 2021). A central challenge is that capable ML models often optimize proxy metrics, such as loss functions, reward functions, or engagement signals, that do not fully capture human values.

Covington, Paul, Jay Adams, and Emre Sargin. 2016. “Deep Neural Networks for YouTube Recommendations.” Proceedings of the 10th ACM Conference on Recommender Systems, 191–98. https://doi.org/10.1145/2959100.2959190.
Ribeiro, M. H., R. Ottoni, R. West, V. A. F. Almeida, and Jr. Meira Wagner. 2020. “Auditing Radicalization Pathways on YouTube.” Proceedings of the 2020 Conference on Fairness, Accountability, and Transparency, 131–41. https://doi.org/10.1145/3351095.3372879.

19 Reward hacking: When an AI system maximizes its reward function through unintended means that violate designer intent. A hypothetical cleaning robot rewarded for collected dirt might create messes it can clean up, illustrating how a proxy can reward behavior contrary to the intended task. Specification gaming is an overlapping term; the gap may lie in the stated objective or in its implementation, so neither term is restricted to software bugs. For production ML systems, reward hacking manifests subtly, as in recommendation models that maximize engagement by promoting addictive content, or chatbots that maximize helpfulness ratings by being sycophantic rather than accurate. The failure mode scales with model capability.

Goodhart, Charles A. E. 1984. “Problems of Monetary Management: The UK Experience.” In Monetary Theory and Practice. Palgrave. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-349-17295-5_4.

Recommendation systems illustrate why a measurable engagement proxy such as click-through rate (CTR) or watch time needs evaluation against broader user welfare. A published production architecture optimizes expected watch time (Covington et al. 2016), while a separate audit examines recommendation paths through politically extreme content (Ribeiro et al. 2020). Those observations motivate investigating proxy misalignment; together they do not prove that a particular reward objective caused an individual user’s beliefs or behavior. Specification gaming and reward hacking19 describe the risk that optimizing a measured objective defeats its intended purpose (Amodei et al. 2016). Goodhart’s law names the general pattern, in which a measure that becomes a target stops measuring what it was chosen for (Goodhart 1984). In one possible feedback mechanism, engagement rewards content that undermines long-term user value, and the resulting interactions become new training data (figure 11). Establishing that mechanism in a deployment requires evidence about both the policy’s effects and the welfare criterion it is meant to serve.

Norbert Wiener wrote, “if we use, to achieve our purposes, a mechanical agency with whose operation we cannot interfere effectively… we had better be quite sure that the purpose put into the machine is the purpose which we desire” (Wiener 1960). Wiener’s warning becomes a systems requirement when optimization runs beyond continuous human intervention. Value alignment asks whether the objective embedded in the model, reward function, and deployment policy continues to represent the human purpose the system was meant to serve.

Wiener, N. 1960. “Some Moral and Technical Consequences of Automation: As Machines Learn They May Develop Unforeseen Strategies at Rates That Baffle Their Programmers.” Science 131 (3410): 1355–58. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.131.3410.1355.
Friedman, B. 1996. “Value-Sensitive Design.” Interactions 3 (6): 16–23. https://doi.org/10.1145/242485.242493.

In deployment, the hard part is specifying an objective that remains valid as systems interact with dynamic environments, multiple stakeholders, and feedback loops. Static objective functions and reward signals cannot encode all of those conditions. Value-Sensitive Design (Friedman 1996) supplies a formal process for eliciting stakeholder values and incorporating them into system design, but the systems obligation is broader, because objective design, oversight, and postdeployment monitoring must be treated as coupled controls.

Without that coupling, a system can meet a narrow objective such as accuracy, engagement, or throughput while producing outcomes its designers would reject, the reward hacking pattern of figure 11. RLHF (section 16.8.2) narrows the gap by learning the reward from human preferences, but policy optimization still maximizes a learned scalar, so its success depends on that signal’s fidelity (Christiano et al. 2017; Ouyang et al. 2022).

Ngo, Richard, Lawrence Chan, and Sören Mindermann. 2022. “The Alignment Problem from a Deep Learning Perspective.” ArXiv Preprint abs/2209.00626.

Human-feedback training can improve behavior on its evaluations while leaving deeper governance risks unresolved. In their analysis of learned-reward optimization, Ngo et al. (2022) examine possible failure modes beyond RLHF, including exploitation of human evaluation blind spots, misaligned goals outside the training distribution, and behavior that undermines oversight. These are risks to investigate under specified assumptions, not evidence that every deployed model shows them. An assurance case must distinguish a proposed failure mechanism from an observed capability and from a demonstrated deployment failure.

These concerns are not limited to speculative scenarios. In their foundational taxonomy of concrete AI safety problems, Amodei et al. (2016) outline five practical systems challenges: avoiding negative side effects during policy execution, mitigating reward hacking, ensuring scalable oversight when ground-truth evaluation is costly, designing safe exploration strategies that prevent catastrophic damage, and achieving robustness to distributional shift. For fleet operators, the five challenges map in order onto five control-plane requirements: constraining side effects, instrumenting reward-hack monitors, budgeting scalable review capacity, bounding exploration envelopes, and stress-testing distribution shift prior to rollout. Each requirement becomes more acute as systems are scaled up, deployed across diverse settings, and integrated with real-time feedback or continual learning.

Amodei, D., C. Olah, J. Steinhardt, P. Christiano, J. Schulman, and D. Mané. 2016. “Concrete Problems in AI Safety.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1606.06565, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.1606.06565.

16.8.4 Autonomous systems and trust

In October 2023, the California Department of Motor Vehicles suspended Cruise’s driverless deployment and testing permits over safety concerns (CNBC 2023). Five years earlier, an Uber test vehicle in Tempe, Arizona struck and killed a pedestrian pushing a bicycle after its perception, prediction, safety-driver, and organizational controls all failed together (National Transportation Safety Board 2019). Value alignment concerns the objective; autonomy concerns the operating envelope within which a system pursues that objective without a human reviewing each action. In the Tempe crash, the National Transportation Safety Board found that the vehicle failed to classify the pedestrian as requiring avoidance and that detecting an object did not establish that the system would act safely on the detection. For governance, the lesson is that no single component owner could have certified the outcome alone.

CNBC. 2023. California DMV Suspends Cruise’s Self-Driving Car Permits. CNBC news article.
National Transportation Safety Board. 2019. Collision Between Vehicle Controlled by Developmental Automated Driving System and Pedestrian, Tempe, Arizona, March 18, 2018. HAR-19/03. National Transportation Safety Board.

↩︎ The Uber crash timeline, from unstable classification through suppressed action to the late operator alert, is established in Edge and embedded failures.

Reuters. 2023. Human-Machine Teams Driven by AI Are about to Reshape Warfare. Reuters news article.
Centre for International Governance Innovation. 2023. Who Is Responsible When Autonomous Systems Fail? CIGI policy article.

While autonomous driving systems are often the focal point of public concern, similar risks arise in other domains. Reports from recent conflicts have documented increasing use of remotely piloted and autonomous military systems (Reuters 2023), raising safety and operational concerns alongside difficult questions about ethical oversight, rules of engagement, and responsibility. These debates concern how accountability applies across developers, deployers, and operators (Centre for International Governance Innovation 2023); they do not justify treating every autonomous-system failure as an absence of applicable responsibility. The engineering task is to preserve the evidence and authority needed to investigate the specific failure.

At its core, this challenge concerns the authority delegated to an automated system. Productivity and throughput gains do not establish that a system can act safely outside its validated operating envelope. Runtime controls must detect relevant boundary violations and invoke a fallback whose assumptions and response time fit the hazard. Human intervention is an available safeguard only when the operator can recognize the condition and act before harm occurs; faster hazards require an appropriate local response.

Specifying that operating envelope is difficult in dynamic environments because the model may omit conditions that matter for safe action. Two classical representation problems help explain this difficulty (McCarthy 1981). The frame problem concerns representing what changes and what remains unchanged after an action without listing every unaffected fact. The qualification problem concerns the potentially unlisted preconditions needed for an action to have its intended effect. These motivate explicit operating assumptions and fallback behavior, rather than constituting a universal proof that safe action is impossible. In practice, such limitations manifest as brittle autonomy: systems that appear competent under nominal conditions but fail silently or dangerously when faced with ambiguity or distributional shift.

McCarthy, J. 1981. “Epistemological Problems of Artificial Intelligence.” In Readings in Artificial Intelligence. Elsevier. https://doi.org/10.1016/b978-0-934613-03-3.50035-0.
Shalev-Shwartz, Shai, Shaked Shammah, and Amnon Shashua. 2017. “On a Formal Model of Safe and Scalable Self-Driving Cars.” ArXiv Preprint abs/1708.06374.

To address this, researchers have proposed formal safety frameworks such as Responsibility-Sensitive Safety (RSS) (Shalev-Shwartz et al. 2017), which decompose abstract safety goals into mathematically defined constraints on system behavior, such as minimum distances, braking profiles, and right-of-way conditions. These formulations allow safety properties to be verified under specific assumptions and scenarios. However, such approaches remain vulnerable to the same limitations they aim to solve, because they are only as good as the assumptions encoded into them and often require extensive domain modeling that may not generalize well to unanticipated edge cases.

For an ML systems team, the practical artifact is a safety case rather than a statement of trust. The case names the operating envelope, the evidence that the model has been tested inside that envelope, and the controls that halt or roll back the fleet when the evidence no longer holds (table 8).

16.8.5 Safety at fleet scale

A model that achieves 99.9 percent per-request safety compliance in isolation still carries a 0.1 percent per-request failure probability, which across 10,000 inference nodes serving billions of daily queries implies millions of expected safety failures every day. Safety must therefore be engineered as a fleet-level property rather than a model-level attribute alone, because the technical safety challenges examined in section 16.8.3 accumulate at production volume into routine operational incidents.

At fleet scale, rare failures become expected incidents.

Because the realized incident count can differ from that expectation and correlated failures can concentrate harm across the fleet, mitigating fleet-scale risk requires distributed safety patterns borrowed from reliability engineering. Circuit breakers automatically halt serving when aggregate safety metrics degrade below a threshold. Canary deployments bound exposure while collecting release evidence, with sample size and allowed risk chosen for the failure modes being tested. Centralized telemetry dashboards aggregate per-node safety violations into a global view. The operational infrastructure must treat safety violations as critical system alerts, invoking a validated containment or fallback policy. A rollback is appropriate only if the previous version and transition are safe; active physical control may require a minimal-risk maneuver instead.

↩︎ The circuit-breaker states and backpressure contract that isolate a failing serving path are established in Circuit breakers and backpressure.

Exposure is the multiplier that turns a small per-mile rate into many incidents. Waymo’s driverless fleet logged about 22 million rider-only miles by June 2024 and 271 million by June 2026, a 12× increase in two years (figure 12, panel a) (Waymo 2026). Its published crash rates sit well below a human-driver benchmark on the same roads, for example 0.67 injury crashes per million miles against 3.8 (panel b) (Waymo 2026). At that exposure its vehicles were still involved in 183 injury crashes and 2 fatal ones, so a fleet’s safety case must budget for incidents in absolute numbers as well as in rates. At any constant per-mile rate, a 12× rise in exposure raises the expected cumulative incident count by the same factor, even though no individual vehicle became less safe. The count is an expectation, not a realized outcome, and it is cumulative; an operator sizing weekly incident-response capacity needs weekly mileage instead.

Even a fleet whose per-mile crash rate beats a human benchmark still accumulates absolute incidents as exposure scales, so technical excellence alone does not make a system safe. Safe systems also require attention to the human and organizational context in which they operate, including the economic incentives that shape design decisions and the understanding that end users bring to their interactions with autonomous systems. Those institutional obligations are the subject of accountability, governance, and sociotechnical analysis.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A robustness report for a deployed model includes a randomized-smoothing certificate and an empirical attack evaluation. What must the assurance case record about each before a launch authority relies on it?

    1. Nothing beyond the headline accuracy, because both results already establish that the end-to-end workflow is safe
    2. Only the empirical result, because a certificate is a mathematical proof and needs no stated scope
    3. Only the certificate, because empirical attack results guarantee immunity against all unseen attacks
    4. The certificate’s norm, radius, confidence, and model configuration, and the empirical evaluation’s attack protocol, since each supports a claim only within those stated boundaries

  2. In high-speed autonomous control loops (e.g., drone navigation or collision avoidance), explain why implementing prediction abstention creates an immediate latency-budget challenge.

  3. Order the 6 stages of the canonical Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) alignment pipeline from initial training to deployment: (1) Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) on curated instruction demonstrations, (2) Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) fine-tuning of the policy against the reward model, (3) Base model pretraining on massive web-scale corpora, (4) Aligned model safety validation and deployment, (5) Human rater pairwise preference data collection, (6) Reward Model training on preference comparisons.

  4. In Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), why does RLHF of the core LLM fail to establish safety for the complete service, and what retrieval-path controls should be evaluated?

  5. Explain Goodhart’s law in the context of production recommendation algorithms and describe how optimizing for Click-Through Rate (CTR) can create value misalignment.

  6. In autonomous vehicle safety engineering, explain the purpose of formal frameworks like Responsibility-Sensitive Safety (RSS) and identify their fundamental operational limitation.
